0,2026-07-26," June 28, 2026 Â· Matt Lenhard Â· 9 min read 
Iâve spent a lot of time thinking about token fraud, a problem I first stumbled upon while working as a software engineer on an AI gateway.
We faced constant abuse. At first it was free-credit abuse, where users spun up accounts en masse. Then it was our support chatbot. I started talking to friends about it and hearing stories of companies losing millions of dollars to abuse each day.
The abuse took a number of different shapes, and the abusers were relentless. I came to realize that the problem was much bigger than us. A new form of fraud had emerged, and it had become endemic to the token economy.
While researching where the abuse was coming from, I stumbled onto a Chinese forum where operators openly discussed the relays and their methods. My notes on the industry and its players are below.
A relay â or âtransfer stationâ â is essentially a service that proxies traffic to U.S. models, often at a deep discount. For example, one operatorâs price-comparison site listed a package that bought the equivalent of $3,333 worth of official Anthropic credit for 425 RMB â roughly $0.13 of usage per $1 spent.
To make that concrete, hereâs how far below official pricing the relays we track actually run, ranked by discount:
The ecosystem runs four layers deep, from the merchants sourcing raw accounts down to the developers buying cheap tokens:
 Card & account merchants â virtual credit cards built to pass U.S. and European billing checks, plus bulk-registered accounts. 
 Account pools â aggregate hundreds of upstream accounts, manage tokens and rate limits, handle failover, and expose a single API. 
 Relays / transfer stations â wrap the pool's API in a clean, billed, Chinese-language product and compete on price. 
 Developers, startups, and SaaS chasing cheap inference â plus commercial buyers running model distillation. 
Sitting at the top are the card merchants (å¡å) and account merchants (å·å). They sell virtual credit cards designed to pass U.S. and European billing checks, along with bulk-registered accounts.
In the middle sit the account pools (è´¦å·æ± ). A pool aggregates dozens or hundreds of upstream accounts, manages their authentication tokens and rate limits, handles failover when accounts get flagged, and exposes a single API surface that downstream relays can consume.
The inventory isnât only model-lab accounts. Alongside direct OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google credentials are accounts harvested from the application layer.
Much of the forumâs activity centers on âreverse-engineeredâ access to tools like Kiro and antigravity, which are consumer products, not lab APIs. To a pool, it makes no difference whether a token comes from a lab or from an app built on one; anything that resells or exposes a model is a target.
Downstream sit the relay / transfer stations themselves â the consumer-facing layer. They wrap the poolâs API in a clean Chinese-language product, handle billing and invoicing, run customer-support WeChat groups, and compete on price.
At the bottom are individual Chinese developers, small startups, and mid-sized SaaS companies hunting for cheap inference â as well as some larger commercial buyers using the infrastructure for model distillation.
In practice these layers blur. Many operators run both the pool and the relay, and the forumâs own participants often use âpoolâ and âtransfer stationâ interchangeably.
Almost every relay Iâve looked at runs on one of two open-source projects: one-api or new-api.
Both are OpenAI-compatible gateways. An operator deploys the panel and adds a set of channels (æ¸ é). Each channel represents a provider plus a pool of API keys. The panel exposes a single endpoint that matches the OpenAI API, so buyers just point their existing SDK at the relayâs URL. On every request it pulls a key from the pool, forwards it upstream, returns the response, and deducts quota priced by usage times a multiplier (åç). It manages the users, tokens, pricing tiers, logs, and billing.
new-api is a more actively developed fork of one-api, and the difference is mostly commerce: it ships with self-service payment and recharge, plus image, video, and audio models. Across the relays we track, one-api  turns up roughly four times as often as new-api; the original base is the more widespread of the two, even if new-api is the one built to sell.
Thereâs nothing inherently illicit about the software. one-api and new-api are neutral, legitimate tools. Plenty of companies self-host them to put their own accounts behind a single gateway with team quotas and spend tracking. A relay crosses the line when its channels are stocked with stolen, leaked, or pooled keys instead of the operatorâs own, and when it resells that access against the providersâ terms.
The three main use cases seem to be cheap tokens, getting around geo-restrictions and model distillation. A few relevant quotes from the forum:
âDistillation uses Claude/CodeX models to train domestic models. There are intermediaries that specialize in distillation and can provide relevant evidence, but I canât name specific domestic companies. Anyway, companies with strong programming capabilities are all distilling Claude; itâs a multi-billion RMB industry chain, and many big players earn hundreds of thousands a day.â
âItâs not just true â many distillers in the industry have made millions.â
âI got 20TB on my first day online.â (And: âYou might get 100TB of traffic as soon as you go online.â)
I was surprised by how mature the market already is. There are price-comparison sites for the relays, affiliate programs, and even gateway products. On the forums, consumer demand looks just as strong. And these arenât fringe operations: the ten highest-traffic relays we track pull a combined 3.6 million visits a month between them.
My hunch is that things get worse for the application layer from here. As Anthropic and others roll out KYC controls and identity verification, the abuse wonât disappear, it will just move somewhere else.
A clear sign of how normalized this has become is that one of the relay directories runs a daily lottery for API keys.
The site â hvoy.ai, which otherwise bills itself as a relay authenticity checker and price-comparison tool, gives away fifty $100 API keys every single day. You earn entry credits from a daily check-in, spend 20 credits per ticket, and can buy up to three tickets a round. On the day I looked, 258 people had entered 401 tickets for the fifty keys.
The part that got me is the fairness theater. The draw is provably fair â the same cryptographic scheme legitimate crypto-gambling sites use to prove they didnât rig the result. The random seed is the hash of the latest Bitcoin block, winners are picked with a Partial Fisher-Yates shuffle, and the full list of entries is published as a snapshot before the draw.
Iâve talked to many companies facing this, and the truth is that thereâs no clean fix. Fraud is a constant cat-and-mouse game. What follows isnât a silver bullet â itâs the set of things that Iâve seen work and that others are doing, roughly in the order that abuse travels: from account creation, to detection and then to damage control.
Assume some abuse gets through anyway, and limit what it can cost you:
And when you do catch someone, throttle quietly. A clean error just tells the attacker which signal to fix before they come back.
None of this stops the abuse for good. But if you make attacking your own service expensive enough that the numbers stop working, theyâll try to find an easier target.
All quotes are translated from a V2EX thread in the siteâs Programmers section, âA comprehensive guide to AI transfer station jargon,â started by the user v2exgo (who operates the relay at terminal.pub). The thread ran from March 5 to June 23, 2026 and drew roughly 35,000 views and 190 replies. Reply numbers below refer to that thread."

3,2026-07-26,"When Hamza Mostafa started studying computer science at university, he pictured going into traditional software engineering at a Big Tech company after he graduated.
As time went on, though, he decided to specialize in AI. In 2024, he landed a three-month internship at OpenAI for the following year.
In an interview with Business Insider, Mostafa, 21, who is based in San Francisco and works on his own AI projects, shared his tips for people who want to take advantage of the AI boom and specialize in the field.
As AI and machine learning are quite broad topics, Mostafa suggested gaining a general understanding of the field before zeroing in on a specialization.
""For me, that was agents,"" he said.
Once you've chosen your specialization, dive deep into it and start building projects around it. This can help identify gaps in your understanding and gain practical knowledge.
""The best way I learn is by doing and by building,"" Mostafa said.
Try a
                                  Level Up action pack.
Build a calm, realistic plan to recover from job loss and move toward your next role.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
These projects can become something concrete to discuss during interviews to show employers you've explored the field, he said.
People who can show they ""understand things at a very deep level"" and follow the three-step formula of gaining a broad understanding, followed by picking a specialization, and building projects, are ""highly employable,"" Mostafa believes.
When building an understanding of the AI field, AI tools like ChatGPT and Claude can help teach key concepts, Mostafa said.
""I still do this every day. I'll go on Claude or ChatGPT and have it explain these concepts to me to make sure I really understand to the deepest level, but also to a level where I can explain it to my mother, for example, who doesn't really understand AI,"" he said.
When Mostafa was beginning to research AI himself, he made use of YouTube, searching for ""intro to AI"" videos that could explain natural language processing and large language models.
""I'm sure there's probably tons of other blogs or resources that are great too,"" he added.
""The tech ecosystem sometimes can be a bit toxic,"" Mostafa said, adding that opening LinkedIn and seeing people talk about the rough job market or the hot job they've landed can be demotivating.
Though he knew he couldn't control the job market, he concentrated on getting his résumé in order and trying to network.
""For me, what really helped was focusing on what's in my control,"" he said, encouraging students and job seekers trying to break into the industry to do the same.
Do you have a story to share about breaking into AI? Contact this reporter at ccheong@businessinsider.com

4,2026-07-26,"In recent years, Macs had been getting more and more affordable. Cheaper starting points, more memory and storage per dollar, and the introduction of MacBook Neo. For the first time, Apple had started to become one of the best values on the market.
Unfortunately, Apple just drastically increased its prices because of the ongoing memory shortage. Macs aren’t as appealing as they were a few weeks ago, that said, there’s still some decent deals out there – and if you’re in need of a new computer, I’d still say todays Macs are a good value against the rest of the computing market.
Up until the launch of MacBook Neo, the MacBook Air represented the entry point to Apple’s laptop lineup. Despite MacBook Neo making its value proposition slightly questionable, I’d still say MacBook Air is a great laptop if you want a better experience without needing anything premium.
MagSafe, a P3 color display, Center Stage on the webcam, and more battery life are just a few of the highlights with MacBook Air. It also comes with a 15-inch size variant, unlike MacBook Neo which only offers 13-inch.
Right now, you can find these discounts across the MacBook Air lineup:
13-inch MacBook Air:
15-inch MacBook Air:
This product doesn’t need a long explanation – MacBook Neo is Apple’s first affordable MacBook, coming in at just $699.
It has some compromises compared to the aforementioned MacBook Air, but it’s still incredibly good for the price. Build quality, display sharpness, and the macOS experience are all essentially uncompromised. Sure, you have to deal with a slower A18 Pro chip (though trust me, it’s no slouch), lesser ports, and 8GB of memory, but it’s perfectly reasonable for the price. It also comes in fun colors like Citrus, Blush, and Indigo.
There’s no notable retail discounts on MacBook Neo right now, though if you’re a student, you can fetch $100 off from Apple. Otherwise, you can buy one on Amazon:
At the top of the lineup is MacBook Pro. You get the best of the best, though there is one major caveat. Apple is reportedly redesigning the high-end variants of MacBook Pro later this year with OLED, touchscreens, and thinner designs.
That redesign will only debut with the M5 Pro and M5 Max chip, though – so if you’re someone buying the base model with the M5 chip, you’re still in the clear to buy a new MacBook today. Same applies if you don’t care about the redesign at all, since M5 Pro and M5 Max are still incredible new chips.

5,2026-07-26,"Sitting shoulder-to-shoulder in plastic chairs, hundreds of defiant San Francisco residents, a noticeable number of them gray-haired, packed themselves into a gallery at the Fort Mason Center last Thursday.
The reason? To protect their famously quaint waterfront neighborhood from the development of a large new residential tower.
San Francisco Chronicle reporter Laura Waxmann captured last week's scene in a photo she shared on X, which has since sparked pushback from social media users frustrated by the city's housing market.
Hundreds of #SanFrancisco Marina residents turned out this evening for townhall on controversial @Safeway housing plan, which would place what for many is an uncomfortably tall, 22 & 18 story project at the low-slung neighborhood’s waterfront. @sfchronicle pic.twitter.com/2MTDL19NyQ
Many of the responses came from the tech industry, which, some might argue, has played a role in San Francisco's housing crisis. They zeroed in on the crowd's appearance and characterized the opposition as highly organized.
Jason Calacanis, an investor, podcaster, and prominent voice in Silicon Valley, was among the chorus of tech workers who, in response to the photo, criticized the residents opposing the new development.
""These oldsters were dropping acid, dancing naked in Golden Gate Park and living on each others couches 60 years ago,"" he wrote on X. ""Now they're banning new housing to protect their $7m Victorians.""
Last year, Calacanis left the city for Texas, where he now owns a 33-acre ranch.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
Evan Conrad, the CEO of the San Francisco Compute Company, a marketplace for AI startups and others to rent and trade large-scale computing power — which was valued at $300 million as of last year — blamed those resisting new residential buildings for the city's lack of affordable housing.
""when rent is $8k for a one bedroom, please remember that the fault is in the people who, for 50 years, showed up to argue against nearly every unit of housing proposed,"" he said on X in response to the photo.
San Francisco Board of Supervisors member Bilal Mahmood said the photo of the meeting symbolized a generational divide.
""We have a deep affordability crisis right now, and younger generations are calling BS now both on NIMBYs who will find any excuse to oppose housing, as well as on our city's lack of action,"" Mahmood wrote in an X post over the weekend, using the acronym for ""not in my backyard.""
The Marina District sits on San Francisco's northern waterfront. It is famous for its park with views of the Golden Gate Bridge, and its Victorian and Mediterranean Revival houses.
Local development firm Align Real Estate has proposed demolishing the current Safeway grocery store on the waterfront to build a new version with a housing complex on top.
The U-shaped complex would include a 20-story and an 18-story tower that combined would house 848 units, according to local outlets. Initial documents related to the project were filed in December 2025.
A proposal to build 790 apartments on the site of a Safeway and surface parking lot in San Francisco’s Marina district would more than double the size of the grocery store. Naturally, the mayor, the district supervisor, and many nearby residents oppose it. pic.twitter.com/8inqnw9SHT
Although the San Francisco Planning Department has not yet approved the proposal, the mixed-use development has drawn fierce opposition from many residents. They are concerned that the development will increase traffic, cause environmental issues, and redefine the neighborhood's character.
""It has become a test of how California's state housing laws affect local planning, environmental review, and whether communities still have a meaningful voice in shaping their neighborhoods,"" a flyer advertising the community meeting said.
That's one side. On the other is younger residents in the tech industry and beyond competing for pricey apartments. They believe more residential units could benefit a city facing an extreme housing shortage and sky-high rents. Many of them also said the divide was between the old and the young.
What's happening in the Marina District is something of a microcosm of the housing crisis across the country. A March 2026 Realtor report found that the housing supply gap surpassed 4 million homes last year.
Older Americans — specifically baby boomers — dominate the country's housing market. Many of them have chosen to age in place or forgo downsizing, leaving fewer options for younger Americans hoping to enter the housing market. That is part of what is forcing millennials and Gen Z to cut costs by renting or moving in with their parents. Rising home costs and mortgage rates are also barriers to entry, as is the lack of newly built housing.
In California, as a result, multigenerational living, or when two or more adult generations live together, is on the rise. Data from Realtor showed that San Francisco ranked high on the list with a 17.40% share of multigeneration listings.

6,2026-07-26,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Philips Hue has been a leader in smart home lighting for quite some time. Designed for plug-and-play connectivity, setting up a Hue system takes no more than a few minutes, and once your lighting tech is activated, you can start adjusting brightness and changing colors pretty much right away. But if you've been shopping around for Philips Hue and weren't a fan of the prices (it's one of the more expensive smart lighting solutions on the market), we have good news: the company also makes a budget-friendly lineup of lighting products, referred to as the Hue Essentials range.
One of the biggest differences between the flagship Philips Hue family and the lesser-priced Hue Essentials is that the former offers deeper lighting customization. For starters, the dimming range of the flagship Philips Hue lineup is greater than what's offered via Hue Essentials; the former can be reduced to as little as 0.2% brightness, while the latter is capped at 2% brightness. Traditional Hue bulbs also have a greater white light range (1,000-20,000K vs. 2,200-6,500K with Hue Essentials).
Think of it this way: if you're just getting into smart home devices, and smart lighting is your first foray into automations, the Hue Essentials range is an excellent introduction. While not as bright or colorful as the flagship Hue lineup, the Essentials tech is no illumination straggler. Plus, both Philips Hue families are 100% compatible with the Hue Bridge and Bridge Pro, which you'll need if you plan on controlling your lighting tech when away from home.
It's also worth mentioning that, regardless of which Philips Hue product line you decide on, both ranges are supported by assistant tools like Alexa, Google Home, and Apple HomeKit. That said, these ecosystem controllers require a Hue Bridge to get all your tech on the same page, and at $70, even the standard Bridge isn't that cheap. If you'd rather wait to invest in a Hue Bridge, you'll still be able to onboard up to 10 Hue bulbs (either type) via Bluetooth and the Hue app.
Fortunately, you'll often be able to find Hue starter kits and smart home bundles at Amazon, Best Buy, and several other retailers. In putting together this article, we came across this $143 Hue starter kit (marked down to $80 at the time of writing), which comes with a Hue Bridge and two A19 bulbs, as well as this $100 Essentials kit. The latter comes with a Hue Bridge and four E26 bulbs. Setting up a Bridge is no big challenge, either; just wire it up to your router, add it to your Philips Hue account, and that's all there is to it.
Before the Hue Essentials lineup was added to the greater Philips arsenal, the manufacturer faced off against numerous budget-friendly smart lights. Companies like TP-Link, Govee, and even Amazon Basics are all solid alternatives, but the Hue Essentials lineup is just as worthy of your hard-earned cash, especially if you already use Hue lighting tech.
Found this useful? Join our free weekly newsletter for smart tech buys and products to avoid."
7,2026-07-26,"By 


      Dhanika Pineda
    

Graduates in the class of 2026 are navigating an unusually choppy labor market. We talked to three fresh grads about their post-grad experience so far.
AYESHA RASCOE, HOST:  Summer break is when college students can finally exhale after a long school year. But if they just wrapped up their final year in school, they might still be holding their breath. Graduates in the class of 2026 are navigating an unusually weak labor market, where unemployment for young workers, age 22 to 27, has risen at nearly double the rate of the rise in overall unemployment. That's according to an analysis from the Economic Policy Institute over the last three years. The National Association of Colleges and Employers found that less than half of the class of 2026 graduated with a job.Some are working jobs that don't require a college degree or internships that they really hope will turn into a job, or are just sitting out the job market for another few years with grad school. We wanted to hear from a few 2026 grads to see how they're faring since walking across the stage, degree in hand.AMBER CHERRY: My name's Amber Cherry. I'm 22 years old, and I studied English, and then I had minors in film and tech, ethics and society. Right now, I'm working on a movie as a locations PA. So that's basically just helping look over the different locations that we'll be shooting and a lot of kind of grunt work, moving around traffic cones. And I think I get a fluorescent vest next week. So that's what I'm doing for the next month.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)CHERRY: It's hard for me to make plans because I have no plan. Plans with friends, traveling, et cetera - kind of hard to do when you don't know when your next job might start up or if they'll need you, like, on the spot, especially in the entertainment industry.And so right now, I'm in Nashville, Tennessee, which is my hometown. Sometimes when I'm home, I start to revert back to the habits that I had when I lived here, you know, or, like, a little bit of my 17-year-old self starts to come out. I've kind of heard online or just in public discourse of, like, no one wants to work anymore, but that's kind of all we want to do. Everyone I know is begging, like, please give me a job. And we're not begging for, like, big jobs. We're begging for entry-level jobs.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)CHERRY: I know a lot of people in my generation can be afraid to, like, call people, but we've had to get over that (laughter), even though that's, like, the last thing my generation wants to do, is call someone on the phone.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)GABBY MATTHEWS: To me, it still feels like, you know, I'm going through the motions. Like, I'm still experiencing my summer. I think it's really going to hit in August when everyone starts going back to school, and it's like, OK, like, I'm genuinely working. Though I think, for me, it's kind of just been a fever dream. I definitely enjoy knowing that I don't have any more homework for a while.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: My name is Gabby Matthews. I studied business management, and I graduated from Howard University, class of 2026.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: I'm currently located in New Jersey, but I also, like, work in New York. So right now, I work within, like, the merchandising room, so I'm currently a buyer trainee. I have family that lives in New Jersey, luckily, so I'm commuting from a relative's house, staying until I find something more permanent.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: The first thing we learned at Howard was the opportunities that you want are absolutely accessible to you, and you have to put the work in to get what you want out of it. Seeing my classmates, you know, get internships in their freshman and sophomore year is definitely something that inspired me to really work hard and be a go-getter and make sure I had something by at least junior year, you know, going to the job fairs. It got to a point I had an Excel sheet set up, and, like, just reflecting back on all the things that I applied to compared to what I even heard back from.And then also just, like, the process of, like, hearing back and going through interviews, it can be very difficult. A lot of people weren't able to get return offers - not only return offers, but, like, jobs. I know a lot of people going to grad school - like, a lot of people going to grad school - and the job market is absolutely crazy right now.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)ERICK AHUMADA: My name is Erick Ahumada. I graduated with my bachelor's degree in computer science.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: I finally got a call back about a return offer for a probationary internship, so it's not even full-time just yet. Like most people, I want a high-paying job - right? - that, you know, is stable. And computer science for a while, was, you know, toted to be that. Easily - you know, make six figures, have a stable, nice job. Out of my limited friend group, like 20-, 30%, you know, have that offer. Seventy percent are in this weird limbo where we're kind of just waiting.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: My family is willing to let me live with them, so it's pretty tight. So my life has been pretty nice.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: College life, I was really - I need to get this job, and nothing takes higher priority than getting this job. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. Their stories were produced by Dhanika Louise Pineda.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

I studied business management, and I graduated from Howard University, class of 2026.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: I'm currently located in New Jersey, but I also, like, work in New York. So right now, I work within, like, the merchandising room, so I'm currently a buyer trainee. I have family that lives in New Jersey, luckily, so I'm commuting from a relative's house, staying until I find something more permanent.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: The first thing we learned at Howard was the opportunities that you want are absolutely accessible to you, and you have to put the work in to get what you want out of it. Seeing my classmates, you know, get internships in their freshman and sophomore year is definitely something that inspired me to really work hard and be a go-getter and make sure I had something by at least junior year, you know, going to the job fairs. It got to a point I had an Excel sheet set up, and, like, just reflecting back on all the things that I applied to compared to what I even heard back from.And then also just, like, the process of, like, hearing back and going through interviews, it can be very difficult. A lot of people weren't able to get return offers - not only return offers, but, like, jobs. I know a lot of people going to grad school - like, a lot of people going to grad school - and the job market is absolutely crazy right now.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)ERICK AHUMADA: My name is Erick Ahumada. I graduated with my bachelor's degree in computer science.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: I finally got a call back about a return offer for a probationary internship, so it's not even full-time just yet. Like most people, I want a high-paying job - right? - that, you know, is stable. And computer science for a while, was, you know, toted to be that. Easily - you know, make six figures, have a stable, nice job. Out of my limited friend group, like 20-, 30%, you know, have that offer. Seventy percent are in this weird limbo where we're kind of just waiting.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: My family is willing to let me live with them, so it's pretty tight. So my life has been pretty nice.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: College life, I was really - I need to get this job, and nothing takes higher priority than getting this job. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. Their stories were produced by Dhanika Louise Pineda.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

Some are working jobs that don't require a college degree or internships that they really hope will turn into a job, or are just sitting out the job market for another few years with grad school. We wanted to hear from a few 2026 grads to see how they're faring since walking across the stage, degree in hand.AMBER CHERRY: My name's Amber Cherry. I'm 22 years old, and I studied English, and then I had minors in film and tech, ethics and society. Right now, I'm working on a movie as a locations PA. So that's basically just helping look over the different locations that we'll be shooting and a lot of kind of grunt work, moving around traffic cones. And I think I get a fluorescent vest next week. So that's what I'm doing for the next month.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)CHERRY: It's hard for me to make plans because I have no plan. Plans with friends, traveling, et cetera - kind of hard to do when you don't know when your next job might start up or if they'll need you, like, on the spot, especially in the entertainment industry.And so right now, I'm in Nashville, Tennessee, which is my hometown. Sometimes when I'm home, I start to revert back to the habits that I had when I lived here, you know, or, like, a little bit of my 17-year-old self starts to come out. I've kind of heard online or just in public discourse of, like, no one wants to work anymore, but that's kind of all we want to do. Everyone I know is begging, like, please give me a job. And we're not begging for, like, big jobs. We're begging for entry-level jobs.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)CHERRY: I know a lot of people in my generation can be afraid to, like, call people, but we've had to get over that (laughter), even though that's, like, the last thing my generation wants to do, is call someone on the phone.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)GABBY MATTHEWS: To me, it still feels like, you know, I'm going through the motions. Like, I'm still experiencing my summer. I think it's really going to hit in August when everyone starts going back to school, and it's like, OK, like, I'm genuinely working. Though I think, for me, it's kind of just been a fever dream. I definitely enjoy knowing that I don't have any more homework for a while.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: My name is Gabby Matthews. I studied business management, and I graduated from Howard University, class of 2026.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: I'm currently located in New Jersey, but I also, like, work in New York. So right now, I work within, like, the merchandising room, so I'm currently a buyer trainee. I have family that lives in New Jersey, luckily, so I'm commuting from a relative's house, staying until I find something more permanent.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: The first thing we learned at Howard was the opportunities that you want are absolutely accessible to you, and you have to put the work in to get what you want out of it. Seeing my classmates, you know, get internships in their freshman and sophomore year is definitely something that inspired me to really work hard and be a go-getter and make sure I had something by at least junior year, you know, going to the job fairs. It got to a point I had an Excel sheet set up, and, like, just reflecting back on all the things that I applied to compared to what I even heard back from.And then also just, like, the process of, like, hearing back and going through interviews, it can be very difficult. A lot of people weren't able to get return offers - not only return offers, but, like, jobs. I know a lot of people going to grad school - like, a lot of people going to grad school - and the job market is absolutely crazy right now.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)ERICK AHUMADA: My name is Erick Ahumada. I graduated with my bachelor's degree in computer science.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: I finally got a call back about a return offer for a probationary internship, so it's not even full-time just yet. Like most people, I want a high-paying job - right? - that, you know, is stable. And computer science for a while, was, you know, toted to be that. Easily - you know, make six figures, have a stable, nice job. Out of my limited friend group, like 20-, 30%, you know, have that offer. Seventy percent are in this weird limbo where we're kind of just waiting.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: My family is willing to let me live with them, so it's pretty tight. So my life has been pretty nice.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: College life, I was really - I need to get this job, and nothing takes higher priority than getting this job. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. Their stories were produced by Dhanika Louise Pineda.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. CHERRY: It's hard for me to make plans because I have no plan. Plans with friends, traveling, et cetera - kind of hard to do when you don't know when your next job might start up or if they'll need you, like, on the spot, especially in the entertainment industry.And so right now, I'm in Nashville, Tennessee, which is my hometown. Sometimes when I'm home, I start to revert back to the habits that I had when I lived here, you know, or, like, a little bit of my 17-year-old self starts to come out. I've kind of heard online or just in public discourse of, like, no one wants to work anymore, but that's kind of all we want to do. Everyone I know is begging, like, please give me a job. And we're not begging for, like, big jobs. We're begging for entry-level jobs.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)CHERRY: I know a lot of people in my generation can be afraid to, like, call people, but we've had to get over that (laughter), even though that's, like, the last thing my generation wants to do, is call someone on the phone.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)GABBY MATTHEWS: To me, it still feels like, you know, I'm going through the motions. Like, I'm still experiencing my summer. I think it's really going to hit in August when everyone starts going back to school, and it's like, OK, like, I'm genuinely working. Though I think, for me, it's kind of just been a fever dream. I definitely enjoy knowing that I don't have any more homework for a while.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: My name is Gabby Matthews. I studied business management, and I graduated from Howard University, class of 2026.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: I'm currently located in New Jersey, but I also, like, work in New York. So right now, I work within, like, the merchandising room, so I'm currently a buyer trainee. I have family that lives in New Jersey, luckily, so I'm commuting from a relative's house, staying until I find something more permanent.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: The first thing we learned at Howard was the opportunities that you want are absolutely accessible to you, and you have to put the work in to get what you want out of it. Seeing my classmates, you know, get internships in their freshman and sophomore year is definitely something that inspired me to really work hard and be a go-getter and make sure I had something by at least junior year, you know, going to the job fairs. It got to a point I had an Excel sheet set up, and, like, just reflecting back on all the things that I applied to compared to what I even heard back from.And then also just, like, the process of, like, hearing back and going through interviews, it can be very difficult. A lot of people weren't able to get return offers - not only return offers, but, like, jobs. I know a lot of people going to grad school - like, a lot of people going to grad school - and the job market is absolutely crazy right now.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)ERICK AHUMADA: My name is Erick Ahumada. I graduated with my bachelor's degree in computer science.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: I finally got a call back about a return offer for a probationary internship, so it's not even full-time just yet. Like most people, I want a high-paying job - right? - that, you know, is stable. And computer science for a while, was, you know, toted to be that. Easily - you know, make six figures, have a stable, nice job. Out of my limited friend group, like 20-, 30%, you know, have that offer. Seventy percent are in this weird limbo where we're kind of just waiting.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: My family is willing to let me live with them, so it's pretty tight. So my life has been pretty nice.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: College life, I was really - I need to get this job, and nothing takes higher priority than getting this job. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. So right now, I work within, like, the merchandising room, so I'm currently a buyer trainee. I have family that lives in New Jersey, luckily, so I'm commuting from a relative's house, staying until I find something more permanent.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)MATTHEWS: The first thing we learned at Howard was the opportunities that you want are absolutely accessible to you, and you have to put the work in to get what you want out of it. Seeing my classmates, you know, get internships in their freshman and sophomore year is definitely something that inspired me to really work hard and be a go-getter and make sure I had something by at least junior year, you know, going to the job fairs. It got to a point I had an Excel sheet set up, and, like, just reflecting back on all the things that I applied to compared to what I even heard back from.And then also just, like, the process of, like, hearing back and going through interviews, it can be very difficult. A lot of people weren't able to get return offers - not only return offers, but, like, jobs. I know a lot of people going to grad school - like, a lot of people going to grad school - and the job market is absolutely crazy right now.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)ERICK AHUMADA: My name is Erick Ahumada. I graduated with my bachelor's degree in computer science.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: I finally got a call back about a return offer for a probationary internship, so it's not even full-time just yet. Like most people, I want a high-paying job - right? - that, you know, is stable. And computer science for a while, was, you know, toted to be that. Easily - you know, make six figures, have a stable, nice job. Out of my limited friend group, like 20-, 30%, you know, have that offer. Seventy percent are in this weird limbo where we're kind of just waiting.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: My family is willing to let me live with them, so it's pretty tight. So my life has been pretty nice.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: College life, I was really - I need to get this job, and nothing takes higher priority than getting this job. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. Now that I'm kind of on this probation period and, you know, on the track to get the job, I feel a lot more at ease. You know, I've been doing things I enjoy more, I guess, instead of doing, for example, LeetCode or interview prep, or, you know, catching up on whatever is the hype for gen AI technology.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)AHUMADA: Given the field and what I'm working on specifically, like, I'm very much so getting exposed every day, like, oh, yeah, they could for sure fire me today. I did go into mathematics 'cause I thought that would be safe, but my truly honest-to-God major I wanted to do was psychology. I genuinely really loved the classes I took, and I was hoping to do that for bachelor's. But even with how horrible it is now, I think I appreciate the fact that I could get a job at all.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)RASCOE: Voices from the class of 2026 - that was Amber Cherry in Nashville, Gabriella-Ann Matthews in New Jersey, and Erick Ahumada (ph) in Los Angeles, California. Their stories were produced by Dhanika Louise Pineda.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

8,2026-07-26,"Microsoft has spent four decades using Windows as one of consumer software's largest public laboratories. Windows is the public-facing storefront, but behind it sits a collection of test tubes: interfaces that talked to you, wallpapers that browsed themselves, and locked-down operating systems that could not run traditional desktop programs. Some experiments graduate into features you use daily. Others are unceremoniously retired once the market delivers its verdict.
Microsoft naturally celebrates the products that worked. I think the failures are more revealing because each one shows exactly what the company believed the future looked like, moments before the market disagreed. Here are five specimens worth digging up.
Learn how Windows evolved over its years, including how it got its name.
Bob shipped in March 1995 as a $99 shell that replaced the Windows desktop with a cartoon living room. Programs were objects on shelves, your calendar hung on the wall, and a cast of animated guides, most famously Rover the dog, walked you through everything in speech bubbles. Melinda French, then a Microsoft marketing manager and later Melinda French Gates, led the product's marketing effort.
Bob demanded 8MB of RAM at a time when many home PCs had considerably less, cost real money, and could feel patronizing to anyone already comfortable with a PC. It was dead within a year. But the lab notes escaped the building. Vincent Connare designed Comic Sans after deciding that Times New Roman looked absurd inside Bob's speech bubbles, although the typeface was completed too late to ship with the program. Rover, meanwhile, was exhumed six years later to front Windows XP's search assistant, where he annoyed a second generation. Bob died; his organs kept getting transplanted.
 In 1997, Microsoft decided the desktop should behave like a webpage. Active Desktop arrived first as an add-on for Windows 95, then became a built-in part of Windows 98. It allowed users to pin live HTML content to the wallpaper, including stock tickers, news channels, weather panels, and other widgets that refreshed automatically during the brief enthusiasm for push media.
The idea was sound, but the execution was not. It added another fragile web-rendering layer to modest PCs, often through slow dial-up connections that could be expensive to leave running. My lasting memory of Active Desktop is the gray error screen offering to restore it. Active Desktop also demonstrated how deeply Microsoft had wired Internet Explorer into Windows, an approach that became central to the company's much larger antitrust problem. The case was not about animated wallpapers, but Active Desktop made the technical integration behind it unusually visible.
Microsoft never abandoned the underlying idea. Live Tiles, taskbar news panels, and Windows 11's Widgets board all revived the concept of continuously updated information living alongside the desktop.
 Windows Vista shipped in 2007 with support for something called SideShow: tiny auxiliary displays, embedded in laptop lids and remote controls, that could show your email, calendar, or music controls without requiring you to open the main screen. Some implementations cached selected information, allowing it to remain available while the PC was asleep or switched off. Glance at the outside of your closed laptop, see your day.
At that time, that was genuinely clairvoyant. Microsoft had sketched the same glanceable-information model now delivered through smartwatch features such as always-on displays and interactive notifications.
Almost nobody built the hardware. Asus shipped the W5Fe with a 2.8-inch SideShow display embedded in its lid, while a few keyboards, remote controls, and other accessories experimented with the platform. The wider ecosystem never materialized, and Microsoft removed SideShow support in Windows 8.1. Manufacturers had to add cost, thickness, power consumption, and software support to solve a problem few buyers knew they had.
Today, your phone's lock screen and smartwatch face perform much of the same glanceable-information role. The concept had arrived before the hardware ecosystem existed to sustain it. It was waiting for screens people would actually carry, and manufacturers would support.
 October 2012: Microsoft launched the Surface RT, running Windows RT, an ARM-based edition of Windows 8. It had the desktop, and it came with a version of Office. What it could not do was run the familiar x86 Windows programs built for Intel and AMD PCs.
Its ARM processor used a different instruction set from an Intel processor, and Windows RT lacked the translation technology found in modern Windows-on-ARM machines. Later versions of Windows on ARM approached compatibility through x86 emulation, but RT had no equivalent escape hatch.
Microsoft also reserved the recompiled desktop environment largely for its own bundled software, pushing third-party developers toward Windows Store apps instead. Users were largely confined to a Windows Store whose shelves remained conspicuously bare.
Look beyond battery life if you plan to use your laptop for gaming or specialized software.
Customers bought a familiar product and got a stranger wearing its clothes. Nine months later, Microsoft recorded a $900 million charge related to unsold Surface RT inventory, one of the most expensive admissions in the company's hardware history.
Yet RT's underlying thesis, that Windows could benefit from efficient ARM processors and longer battery life, was not wrong. Today's Snapdragon-powered Windows laptops revisit that ambition with far better compatibility, including support for native ARM apps and emulation for many x86 and x64 programs. RT failed because it arrived without the one thing that makes Windows difficult to replace: everything people already expect to run on it.
 Announced in October 2019 alongside the dual-screen Surface Neo, Windows 10X was a simplified version of Windows designed for a new category of folding devices. Built around Microsoft's modernized Windows foundations, it was intended to reduce some of the complexity and legacy behavior associated with conventional PCs.
Microsoft later redirected it toward conventional single-screen devices as demand for familiar laptops surged during the pandemic. After further delays and strategy changes, the company shelved Windows 10X rather than releasing it as a separate product. In May 2021, Microsoft said parts of its technology would be incorporated elsewhere. The Surface Neo never shipped at all.
Except you may be looking at its influence right now. When Windows 11 appeared weeks later, its centered taskbar, simplified Start menu, rounded surfaces, and cleaner visual language strongly echoed the abandoned system. Microsoft never presented Windows 11 as a renamed version of 10X, but the family resemblance was difficult to miss. The product disappeared; parts of its design vocabulary escaped.
Place the five experiments side by side and a clear pattern emerges. Microsoft's failed experiments rarely disappear completely. The interface, hardware idea, or technical ambition is stripped for parts and returned later in a form the market is better prepared to accept.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

9,2026-07-26,"When Cristina Estupiñán was job searching after a layoff, potential employers would ask about her enthusiasm for AI during interviews.
Unfortunately, that enthusiasm was lacking.
""I have to make up something about what excites me about AI because, frankly, nothing really excites me about AI,"" Estupiñán, who worked in software development, previously told Business Insider.
As more companies adopt AI tools — and more job postings mention the technology — employers are asking candidates to discuss AI during interviews. Job seekers don't necessarily have to become AI evangelists, hiring experts say, though they should be prepared to explain how they've used the technology and how they think about it.
Like any other interview topic, it's worth practicing how you'll describe your experience with AI — or explain why you haven't used it much.
What has your experience with AI been like during your job search? Were you asked about your AI usage, or did you have an interview with an AI recruiter? Reach out to these reporters to share at tparadis@businessinsider.com and mhoff@businessinsider.com.
Candidates should expect interviewers to ask how they've used AI in previous roles, how comfortable they are with it, and where they see it fitting into their work. Gallup survey results showed that the share of US employees using AI at work increased from 21% in the second quarter of 2023 to 50% in the first quarter of 2026.
Hiring managers want to understand whether and how a prospective employee used AI, said Kareem Osman, vice president and market director of technology talent solutions at the staffing firm Robert Half.
Try a
                                  Level Up action pack.
Build a calm, realistic plan to recover from job loss and move toward your next role.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
He said candidates can prepare by thinking about a challenge they had, how they used AI to overcome it, and the ultimate outcome.
""Highlighting those tangible results whenever possible is going to be something crucial with these sorts of questions,"" Osman said.
He added that employers place a lot of emphasis on practical application and business impact, so candidates should think about how they streamlined processes, sped up tasks, and supported decision-making.
""Employers are really trying to see individuals that are adaptable, that are embracing new technologies,"" Osman said, adding that hiring managers may also want candidates who can use AI without relying on it entirely. Human judgment still matters in producing the final work, he said.
Judgment calls and interpersonal skills are becoming increasingly important in the tech world. The era of hiring developers mainly for their coding ability is fading, Osman said, as companies seek out well-rounded engineers. Employers want people who can clearly communicate their work to others — including explaining how they use AI tools — and understand business needs.
Job candidates can also use the interview to learn about the employer's approach to AI. Asking how the company uses the technology, what tools employees rely on, and what expectations exist for day-to-day use demonstrates inquisitiveness, Osman said.
""It shows curiosity and to see where the company's headed,"" he said, particularly if AI was a prominent topic in the interview.
Candidates who are skeptical of AI don't need to pretend otherwise, said Vicki Salemi, a career expert with Monster. Instead, she recommends answering questions about the technology honestly and succinctly.
""Brevity is key without being curt about it,"" Salemi said.
At the same time, candidates who haven't used AI much don't have to apologize for it. She said they can explain that they haven't had many opportunities to do so in their roles before asking interviewers how the tech fits into the team's work. That approach, Salemi said, shows candidates are willing to learn without overstating their experience or enthusiasm.
If job seekers' concerns center on a particular area — such as the risks AI might pose to creative work — she said it's reasonable to instead emphasize ways they're comfortable using the technology, such as administrative tasks, scheduling, or logistics.
""You can focus on the things where you don't have a moral problem with it,"" Salemi said.
Being honest about limited experience is one thing. Making opposition to AI the focus of an interview is another, said Josh Bersin, an HR analyst and consultant.
If a job seeker tells a recruiter they don't believe in AI, ""you may have just lost your opportunity to get a job there,"" he said.
Ultimately, Bersin said candidates shouldn't assume employers have AI all figured out, either. Many companies are still experimenting with where the technology adds value, making it worthwhile to ask questions rather than write them off, he said.
""You might get into a conversation with a company that's more interesting than you realized,"" Bersin said.
Candidates who remain skeptical of AI can still position themselves as strong hires, said Mohit Bhende, CEO and cofounder of the talent-evaluation platform Karat, by emphasizing qualities such as judgment, emotional intelligence, creativity, and problem-solving.
""People can thrive, even if they do have that disposition,"" he said.
As AI becomes a more commonplace workplace tool, he expects interviews to focus less on AI itself and more on how candidates think through problems.
""Demonstrate things that you can do that make yourself uniquely able,"" Bhende said.

11,2026-07-26,"Ew, I could never be a cruise person. Or so I thought.
Then I had kids and realized my ideal vacation of wandering cobblestone streets, enjoying a cocktail at sunset, or booking an 8 p.m. dinner reservation at a restaurant that definitely doesn't have a kids' menu, is the opposite of relaxing when you're going to have small children by your side. Honestly, we just need chicken fingers and a waterpark, and we're good.
So when my friend texted asking if I wanted to take the kids on Royal Caribbean's Star of the Seas, one of the world's biggest cruise ships, over a school break, I was immediately in, and so was another friend. Our husbands all had to work and the kids were begging to go.
As someone who appreciates nice resorts, I was more than happy to shell out thousands (a room for four cost us $5,759, plus our flights from LA to Orlando) for a ship that actually lives up to the hype.
Planning a week-long girls' trip with two other moms and seven children between us is a lot to take on, but cruising eliminated much of the usual hassle. 
Food and entertainment are included, so there are no bills to split, and you can book dinner reservations and shows together via the Royal Caribbean app. The ship is a moving hotel too, so you only unpack once. On this voyage, we visited St. Thomas, St. Maarten, and Royal Caribbean's private island, CocoCay, in the Bahamas, which the kids were obsessed with.
Hot tip: Skip the Royal Caribbean excursions and DIY it. It's cheaper, and you never know what mood the kids will be in. We decided to wing it for sanity's sake, which turned out to be the right call since the kids could only handle one stop per island before melting down.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.
Grabbing a taxi at the port is easy and cheap, and we weren't stuck exploring on someone else's schedule. The one exception: the Thrill Waterpark tickets at CocoCay are worth booking in advance. Our 7- and 9-year-olds spent the entire day there and didn't want to leave.
Star of the Seas is newer, cleaner, and frankly way nicer than I ever imagined a cruise ship could be. To me, it was good as, if not better than, some of the five-star, all-inclusive family resorts we've stayed at in Mexico. The rooms are modern and spacious, and Adventure Ocean, the kids' club, is open and free until 10 p.m., so the other moms and I were able to actually enjoy a meal in the main dining room with some peace. I'm also still dreaming about the gooey chocolate dessert I had one night.
The food, in general, genuinely surprised us. The Aquadome Market, a food hall on the ship, has rotating international options that rival LA's best food trucks, and there's a solid self-serve Mexican buffet by the main pool. Because kid favorites are available to grab and go around the ship, we never had to force our often-exhausted kids into a nice sit-down meal. The convenience alone is worth it.
Between seven pools, ten hot tubs, six waterslides, a carousel, and soft-serve ice cream at nearly every turn, the kids were never bored. Neither were the moms. Torque, the aqua show, is a stunning Vegas-caliber production. We loved it so much we went twice and still talk about it.
Vacationing with mom friends means you can tag team the parenting, and when the kids have friends and waterslides, they're not busy bugging you. We're already planning next year's trip and recruiting more moms to join us. One of my friends is even researching matching cruise bags for all of us to use. I guess we're cruise people now.

        2026-07-24
      

where delays are shown to be counterintuitive.

Leaving the house (and the country) for a week or two during the holiday season is a great time to get out of my stereotypical ways of passing free time (entirely too much YouTube, Lobste.rs, HackerNews and BlueSky) and try something else.
For my recent vacation I have packed some grid paper to make a bitmap font, and two books: the first one was Nachové pustiny: a Czech post-apocalyptic novel to get some inspiration for my “Fallout but in Czech Republic” MMO game, and the other was Thinking in Systems: a book recommended at work (and bought with my work education budget) which was collecting dust in my bookshelf for a good few months now.

I’m happy to announce I managed to get through all three of these goals: I’ve created a bitmap font (though I still have kerning and accents to go through), devoured the post-apo novel (it was okay), and got through the systems book!
Thinking in Systems introduces a framework and a visual language of sources and sinks, stocks and flows, feedback loops and systems that (more often than not) emerge bottom-up from them.
It all roughly corresponds to mathematical models and differential equations, though the book doesn’t go into detail of those and only mentions the actual formulas for its examples in an appendix.

There was one really cool chapter that IMHO is the highlight of the whole book. It concerns delays.
The running example (see image above) is a car dealership manager shepherding a stock of cars on their parking lot, aiming for it to always be 10x the amount of cars sold. When the customer demand rises, she starts ordering more cars to cover the gap.

In an ideal world there are no delays: she immediately sees an increased demand and the discrepancy between the current stock and the ideal one, she immediately sends an order to bring more cars in, and the cars immediatelly arrive.
Also, in this example the time granularity is days, so imagine that at the end of the day she sees 30 cars got sold, sends an order for 30 cars and they arrive the next morning.
Let’s first see the constants in our model that won’t be affected by the delays:

The customer demand starts at 20 cars each day, then ramps up to 22 cars each day, with a spike of 70 cars on one specific day.
Sales are min(customer demand, inventory), which in all examples below will equal customer demand, as we have enough cars ready, but you can imagine getting out of stock if demand gets high enough fast enough.
Our manager calculates the desired inventory as sales * 10. Might be simplistic but hey, it’s an example.
You could also imagine us later averaging over sales from past N days and basing the desired inventory off of that, and thus introducing yet another delay into the system, but I’ve omitted it from this blogpost as it doesn’t touch what I want to illustrate.
So, how does the delay-less model behave?

BTW, I have written a Monte Carlo simulator myself in Elm while reading the book—it lists the pseudocode formulas for the examples in this blogpost—but a spreadsheet would suffice, and there are other tools to deal with these differential equation models. Heck, there’s a free graphical webapp built on top of a JS library showing off the same example I do in one of its examples. I guess the book is influential.
This model has no delays, but it has an issue with overreacting to the random spikes in customer demand by buying too many cars which then take forever to sell, and more importantly it’s unrealistic: in the real world everything has a bit of a delay. The orders take time to process, the cars take time to arrive at the dealership lot, and so on. We’ll call that the delivery delay.

That seems like a bad delay, but delays don’t need to always be bad: consider that the manager might want to not overreact to those random spikes. She had ordered 550 cars because of a spike, when she normally orders only around 20 a day! Instead she might want to get to the desired inventory slowly over time by ordering only half of the discrepancy, or a third, etc. If on the next day things get back to normal, we’ll be able to ride the wave much more smoothly without overreacting. Let’s call this divisor the response delay.

Let’s run the model with these two delays and see:

What? What just happened? We thought we’re doing everything right, the response delay was a good thing!
Well it is. But the combination of the two delays made our system oscillate! It never stabilizes even if the customer demand remains constant afterwards.
The manager is caught in a vile cycle of ordering too many cars, then ordering more and more the following days before the first order arrives. Once orders start arriving, she now has too many cars and ends up not ordering any more while waiting for the inventory to get back to the ideal levels. But then with more car sales the inventory dips below the ideal again and she starts the cycle again.
I’d be interested in learning what, mathematically, makes these oscillations inevitable. Some more math-heavy book on these models and differential equations will probably explain that. For now let’s just take the oscillations caused by delays for a fact.
Now you might think that since delays got us into this mess, we should minimize them as much as possible. Shorten the feedback loop and all. But remember, we introduced the response delay to be more resilient towards random spikes. Anyways, let’s try and see what happens:

Hm. As you can see, trying to be faster made things worse: our inventory of cars now oscillates much more: cycles of shooting up from 132 to 518, while we’d like to be around 220. Not to mention having to have 1160 cars in our lot at once after the spike! This is even worse than the no-delays scenario! Is there any hope?
Just to see what will happen: let’s simulate a more patient manager: one that spreads out the restocking over a longer period of time. Let’s make our response delay divisor larger and try 6. When we see a discrepancy of 30 cars at the end of the day, let’s just order 5 instead of 10 or 15 or 30.

Wow! The oscillations died out and stabilized!
This was pretty counterintuitive to me when I read it. Delays intuitively seem like something you want to minimize. But here, shortening a delay made things worse and making it longer made things more predictable and less wasteful.
In the later parts of the book Donella Meadows mentions short feedback loops; there’s no clear cut rule that you could apply blindly. Make a model of your system, try different parameters, see how it behaves.
Many managers and leaders take the lever that the researchers provide, and crank it full speed, wrong direction. You might think you know which way is the correct one, but maybe, just maybe, get insight into how your system would behave in both directions and act on data instead of on intuition.
Feel free to play around with the toy simulator for this example. The code is at Github."

Weekly poll Samsung

Samsung believes that foldables are the future – and, perhaps, that wide models like the new Galaxy Z Fold8 are the future of foldables. What do you think?
Starting with the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold8, despite its plain name, this is a brand new category for the Galaxy foldable family. And while this isn’t the first wide foldable to hit the market, it’s the one that will have the widest reach on the global market. At least until Apple releases its first foldable, which is rumored to be a wide one.
We have a hands-on review with the Z Fold8, plus an in-depth comparison of what sets it apart from the Z Fold8 Ultra. For more, check out our coverage wrap-up post.
Speaking of, the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra is the new name of the big book-style foldable – in other words, this is the successor to the Z Fold7, not the Z Fold8. What did this phone bring to deserve the “Ultra” name? Using Silicon-Carbon, Samsung bumped up the battery capacity to 5,000mAh, 600mAh more than the 2025 Fold. And wired charging is now up to 45W.




Samsung Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra
However, the only camera upgrade on this Ultra phone is a 50MP ultra-wide (up from 12MP). The outdated 3x 10MP telephoto module remains. The 200MP camera does add “Ultra” flair to this phone, though the Z Fold7 had it too. Anyway, it’s more than the new Z Fold8 has – that one only has a 50MP main, a 50MP ultra-wide and no telephoto camera.
The Samsung Galaxy Z Flip8 got slimmer (13.1mm folded, 6.1mm unfolded vs. 13.7mm folded, 6.5mm unfolded) and a touch lighter (180g vs. 188g). And it got a new chipset, the Exynos 2600, with the exception of the US, Canada and China where it gets the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 for Galaxy (its siblings only use the Snapdragon). The Flip missed out on a battery upgrade, leaving it as the only premium Galaxy phone to feature slow 25W charging other than the vanilla Galaxy S26.




Samsung Galaxy Z Flip8
Let’s start with the new model, the Galaxy Z Fold8. According to rumors, Samsung expects this to be the best-selling Z phone of the three. What do you think?
Perhaps you will stick with the classics, i.e. the Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra. Last year, the big book-style foldable outsold the Flip, we’ll see how it fares against the newcomer.
Finally, the Samsung Galaxy Z Flip8 may be the last Flip, if rumors are to be believed. Do you think that the Flip is past its prime or is this just the phone that you want?
Just to make things extra clear, which of the three Galaxy Z8 models do you want?

The National Institutes of Health and the National Science Foundation, along with smaller agencies such as the Energy Research and Development Administration (now the Department of Energy) made the United States the leader in scientific research and development the world had never seen. The Chinafication of Walmart
https://www.npr.org/2026/07/21/nx-s1-5888726/the-chinafication-of-walmart

What Happens When When Fake Oil Prices Meet Reality? Hard Times.  
This article was very educational about how to manipulate paper oil prices.  This could probably be applied to any market, like the stock market.  The government just gets 1 or more big trading houses to take both sides of a large overnight trade (when volume is low) and executes a trade at a target price.  This big trade then sets a trend for humans and algorithms to follow during the following day.  The trading houses are granted immunity for violating regulations.  Then you would not need the Fed with its unlimited money printing ability to execute something like the Greenspan Put.

 KUWAIT: Kuwait has ratified a broad defense cooperation agreement with Pakistan, three years after it was signed in June 2023. The agreement was approved through decree-law no. 73 of 2026 and published in the official gazette Kuwait Al-Youm on Sunday.
